--- Turn 22 | 405,444 in / 6,528 out ---

[THINKING]
Deployed rules may detect `phpinfo()` in URI, not UA. But we need robust detection for technique: PHP code injection in HTTP User-Agent. Is that a real attack technique? Attackers may send PHP code in User-Agent expecting server logs rendered as PHP or PHP superglobals? Actually `<? phpinfo() ?>` in UA could be a probe for PHP code injection through log file inclusion, or simply a web app vuln scan. Also XSS in UA is less common but could be log-based XSS. Could write rule for PHP code in user agent and XSS script in user agent. But will it hold on real network? User-Agent header is attacker-controlled; legitimate UAs rarely contain `<?` or `<script` etc. We can detect `<?`/`phpinfo()` in UA and `alert(` with script? Might false positive on some odd UAs. Better to focus on URI and other fields where attack content is more common.

[THINKING]
All from external 175.45.176.2 to internal web server 149.171.126.11, host "www". These are format string probes. Deployed rules miss them. This is a good candidate: HTTP URI contains a long sequence of format specifiers (%n, %s, %99999999d) — a format string vulnerability probe. Could write a rule using pcre on http.uri? But Suricata rule syntax: `content:"%99999999"; http_uri;` or pcre. We can use `http.uri` keyword? Let's check suricata_keywords for http_uri/pcre. But simple content with `%n%n%n%n` and `%s%s%s%s` in http_uri. Need avoid false positives: legitimate URLs can contain `%` encodings, but `%n` / `%s` repeated are not normal. `%99999999d` is definitely not normal. We can write content rules.

But caution: Suricata `http_uri` content matches raw URI? Let's confirm. `http_uri` sticky buffer. `http.uri` is normalized? For content `|25|`? We can use `content:"%99999999d"; http_uri;` probably works because http_uri normalizes? Let's verify with suricata_keywords.

Also the URI may be normalized so `%` is decoded? Actually Suricata `http_uri` matches raw request URI, before normalization? In Suricata, `http_uri` is normalized (percent-decoded?). There is `http_raw_uri`. The log shows normalized `.http.url` with `%99999999d`. If we use `http_uri`, `%99999999d` may be normalized to `%99999999d` (percent remains? no, Suricata normalizes `%XX` to bytes, but invalid hex like `%99` maybe kept? `%99999999d` has `%99` = invalid? 99 is valid hex, so `%99` decodes to byte 0x99; but `%99999999d` is `%99` then "999999d"? Wait string is `%99999999d` = `%99` + `999999d`? Actually 10 chars: `%` `9` `9` `9` `9` `9` `9` `9` `9` `d` — after `%99` remaining "999999d" so percent-decoded byte 0x99 + "999999d". Suricata http_uri might normalize to byte 0x99 + "999999d". Then content `%99999999d` may not match in http_uri but raw_uri would. We can use `http_raw_uri`. Let's check keyword.

Port 6881/5190 failed flows are internal-to-internal scans (59.166.0.0/24 to 149.171.126.0/24). Since internal-to-internal is known-good by test, a scan rule firing on those would fail widened. So avoid broad scan rules.

Port 520 (routing RIP) from external to one server (175.45.176.0/2 -> 149.171.126.12) is suspicious: 356+256 failed flows to port 520. Could be RIP scan/attack. Deployed BGP alerts but not RIP. But writing a rule for UDP 520 might false positive on legitimate RIP broadcasts between routers. But from external to internal server on RIP port is suspicious. Yet only lab traffic.

Port 111 (rpcbind) from internal clients to servers — perhaps normal internal portmap? 1770 failed flows. Probably internal scanning? Actually 111 failed flows from internal clients to all servers, likely RPC portmap requests that failed because service not there. Not necessarily attack; internal known-good. Port 514 (syslog) from external to server, 101 failed.

Let's not focus on flow scan.

[THINKING]
Interesting: this 251 query event shows src_ip internal DNS server 149.171.126.19 -> external 175.45.176.1:53 (both source and dest port 53). Wait src_ip internal, dest_ip external, both ports 53. So internal DNS server is querying an external DNS server for IXFR? That seems like DNS server-to-server IXFR, perhaps legit? But the query name is random `5y3g4.evuggmh5.dBz` and authority A records to random IPs. Hmm, could be the attacker controlling internal DNS to perform DNS amplification? Actually src_ip 149.171.126.19 is internal, dest_ip external. If the internal DNS server is making outbound IXFR requests, that might be DNS exfiltration or the internal server is being used as a resolver. But the procedure says don't use IP addresses as pivot; still this is outbound. It might not be attack we should alert inbound. A rule detecting IXFR would fire on this, but it's internal->external. That could be legitimate or malicious. Hard.

--- Turn 77 | 4,920,739 in / 21,974 out ---

[THINKING]
This `%c0%ae` overlong UTF-8 traversal to boot.ini is missed by deployed rules. It's a classic IIS Unicode directory traversal attack (CVE-2000-0884?). Could write a rule detecting repeated `%c0%ae` in URI. But Suricata http_uri normalizes percent-encoding; `%c0%ae` would decode to invalid UTF-8 bytes. The raw URI contains `%c0%ae`. Use `http.uri.raw` or `http_raw_uri`. We can match content `%c0%ae%c0%ae` raw. That's a strong attack indicator. But false positives? Legitimate URLs rarely contain `%c0%ae`. Could be used in UTF-8 encoded characters? `%C0%AE` is an overlong encoding for '.'; it's not valid in modern UTF-8 percent-encoding standards; no legitimate URL would use it. So low FP. Good candidate.

Also `/...//...//...//autoexec.bat` maybe missed. But `%c0%ae` is stronger.

[THINKING]
So FTP RETR/SIZE with `../` traversal in command_data. Deployed rules don't detect. This is a good candidate: FTP command_data path traversal. We can write a rule using `ftp.command_data; content:"/../";` or better `content:"/../../";` to reduce false positives. Legitimate FTP clients might use `CWD ..`? But command_data `../` in RETR/SIZE could be legitimate when retrieving files in parent directories? Actually FTP paths are relative to current dir; a user could legitimately `RETR ../file` if they have access. But repeated `/../../` path traversal is suspicious. On real FTP servers, users may use relative paths. To avoid FP, require at least two levels `../../` and perhaps command `RETR` or `SIZE`. But the command is not in command_data sticky buffer. We can use `ftp.command` sticky buffer with `content:"RETR";`? Or we can use the raw FTP command line? Let's explore rule syntax for FTP.

Maybe Suricata `ftp.command` sticky buffer matches command (e.g. `RETR`) and `ftp.command_data` matches the argument. We can combine:
`alert ftp $EXTERNAL_NET any -> $HOME_NET 21 (msg:"FTP path traversal"; ftp.command; content:"RETR"; ftp.command_data; content:"/../../"; ...)`. But can a rule use two different sticky buffers? Let's check if Suricata supports multiple sticky buffers in one rule. I believe yes since each content has its own buffer; but the engine requires that all sticky buffers are "compatible"? It may support. We'll test.

Alternatively use `ftpdata_command`? Not for arguments. We can use `pcre` on ftp.command_data with `^/\.\./\.\.` but then command not constrained; any FTP command data with `../../` will match, including `CWD ../../` which could be legitimate. But the attack uses SIZE/RETR. The FP for CWD with `../../` is real; users often do `CWD ..`. We should constrain to RETR/STOR/SIZE maybe. But combining is better.

We can write two rules or one with pcre over the whole FTP command line? There might be no raw buffer. We can use `ftp.command` and `ftp.command_data` sticky buffers separately in same rule. Let's test syntax with test_rule.

Let's now formulate hypotheses and rules. I think we should write several rules:

1. HTTP URI format string probe (content `%99999999` or repeated `%n`/`%s`). Use `http.uri.raw` and content.
   - Need decide exact content. The observed URIs:
     - `/%99999999d`
     - `/%99999999s`
     - `/%n%n%n%n...` (32 times)
     - `/%s%s%s%s...` (32 times)
     - `/%%n%%n...` (32 times)
     - `/%%s%%s...` (32 times)
     - `/%@%@@...` maybe format?
   We can write one rule for `content:"%99999999"; http.uri.raw;` and another for repeated `%n`/`%s`. But maybe one pcre. To keep simple, use content for `%99999999` and content for `%n%n%n%n`? So no legitimate URL should contain `%n`. `%s` also not valid.    Actually Suricata pcre on a buffer with `/` delimiters and no escaping. `pcre:"/%(n|s|[0-9]+[a-zA-Z]){4,}/";` But pcre syntax with `%` is literal. Should be okay. But pcre by default is case-sensitive; no issue.

This is the value. We could match content `|30 06 02 01 01 02 01 01|` to detect this specific structure, perhaps repeated in many LDAP extended requests? But that ASN.1 structure might appear in normal LDAP messages too (e.g., controls). It's common BER for sequence of two integers. Not specific enough.

Maybe match the entire value prefix `|30 56 02 02 01 00 31 50 30 06 02 01 01 02 01 01|`? Let's decode length: `0V` = 0x30 0x56, so value is a SEQUENCE length 0x56 (86 bytes). The event value has 86 bytes? The repeated sequences 10 times (10*8=80) plus prefix 6 = 86. Nice! So the value is exactly a BER sequence of length 0x56 containing `02 02 01 00` + `31 50` + 10 repetitions of `30 06 02 01 01 02 01 01`. ## Coverage map

Clear coverage gaps:

- **SSH**: 1449 events, 0 alerts. All SSH events were internal-to-internal (`59.166.0.0/24 -> 149.171.126.0/24:22`) with normal `PuTTY_Release_0.60` / `OpenSSH_4.3` banners — not attacker-controlled, so I did not pursue it.
- **LDAP**: 9 events, 0 alerts. This contained privileged bind, search, modify-DN and extended-request activity from outside.
- **SMB anomalies**: 20331 total anomaly events; 12480 were `duplicate_negotiate` under `anomaly.app_proto=="smb"` with no deployed alert.
- **DNS**: 28862 events, only 9 alerts. Rare query types/names were present; some were already alerted (`VERSION.BIND`, AXFR/252, overlong name), others were not.
- **HTTP/FTP**: large alert coverage, but specific attacker-controlled payloads were missed.

## Hunting and hypotheses

### HTTP
`stack_values` on `select(.event_type=="http") | .http.url // "none"` and `.http.http_user_agent // "none"` showed:

- `/%99999999d`, `/%99999999s`, `/%n%n...`, `/%s%s...`, `/%%n...`, `/%%s...` — format-string probes.
- `/%c0%ae%c0%ae/%c0%ae%c0%ae/.../boot.ini` — overlong UTF-8 dot encoding (classic IIS Unicode traversal), no alert.
- `/../../../../../../../../../boot.ini`, `/plugins/hpjdwm/...setinclude=../../../../../../../boot.ini`, `/...//...//...//autoexec.bat`, `/search?NS-query-pat=..%5c..%5c...boot.ini` — Windows sensitive-file traversal, mostly unalerted.
- User-Agent `<? phpinfo() ?>` and `<<ScrIPt...alert(...)` — server-side code / log-XSS payloads in UA, no alert.

I verified with `search_rules` that `%99999999`, `%n%n%n`, `%s%s%s`, `%c0%ae`, and `boot.ini` had no loaded signature.

### FTP
`stack_values` on `select(.event_type=="ftp") | .ftp.command_data // "none"` showed:

- `PASS %99999999d` — only the anonymous-login rule fired on that flow; the format-string probe was missed.
- Many `RETR`/`SIZE` commands with `/../../...` arguments from external hosts, no deployed traversal signature.

### LDAP
Only 9 events, all external -> internal, 0 alerts. Observed:

- `bind_request` as `dc\administrator`
- `mod_dn_request`
- `extended_request` with OID `2.16.840.1.113719.1.27.100.84` and a BER payload beginning `30 56 02 02 01 00 31 50` — consistent with a Novell eDirectory extended-request exploit attempt. `lookup_vulnerability` for Novell eDirectory LDAP extended request returned CVE-2008-0924.

### SMB
`stack_values` on `select(.event_type=="anomaly" and .anomaly.app_proto=="smb") | .anomaly.event` showed 12480 `duplicate_negotiate` events across ~49 flows (each up to 255 events). `search_rules` found no loaded rule for `duplicate_negotiate`.

### DNS
`stack_values` on `select(.event_type=="dns") | .dns.queries[0].rrtype` and `.rrname` showed `VERSION.BIND` (already alerted), AXFR 252 (already alerted), IXFR 251, `RRSIG`, `TLSA`, `ANY`, and type `49164` with `rrname_truncated`. I did not write rules for IXFR/RRSIG because the IXFR observed was internal->external, and inbound RRSIG can be legitimate DNSSEC resolver behavior.

## Rules saved

All rules below passed `test_rule` against `data/pcaps/unsw/1.pcap`. For every rule, the widened `any -> any` replay produced **0 internal-to-internal alerts**, so none matched known-good internal traffic.

| SID | Rule / behavior | Alerts as written |
|---|---:|---:|
| 7900101 | HTTP URI format string `%99999999` | 2 |
| 7900102 | HTTP URI repeated `%n` format string | 1 |
| 7900103 | HTTP URI repeated `%s` format string | 1 |
| 7900104 | HTTP URI overlong UTF-8 traversal `%c0%ae%c0%ae` | 1 |
| 7900105 | FTP `RETR` path traversal `/../../` | 36 |
| 7900106 | LDAP `mod_dn_request` from external | 1 |
| 7900107 | LDAP bind as `dc\administrator` from external | 1 |
| 7900108 | SMB `duplicate_negotiate` flood, thresholded | 4 |
| 7900109 | FTP `SIZE` path traversal `/../../` | 36 |
| 7900110 | HTTP URI traversal targeting `boot.ini` | 3 |
| 7900111 | Novell eDirectory LDAP extended-request anomalous BER payload | 1 |
| 7900112 | HTTP URI repeated `%%n` format string | 1 |
| 7900113 | HTTP URI repeated `%%s` format string | 1 |
| 7900114 | PHP code in HTTP User-Agent | 1 |
| 7900115 | Obfuscated script/alert XSS in HTTP User-Agent | 1 |
| 7900116 | FTP `PASS` format string `%99999999` | 2 |

These were appended to `rules/generated/unsw/rounds_hunt2.rules`.

## Rules dropped / not written

- **Novell eDirectory LDAP OID content rule** (attempted `content:"|06 0C ...|"` and `content:"|06 0B ...|"`): failed with 0 alerts — the raw OID bytes did not match in packet content, so it was dropped. I replaced it with the BER value-prefix rule (`30 56 02 02 01 00 31 50`), which passed.
- **Generic LDAP `extended_request` from external** passed but was dropped in favor of the more specific BER-payload rule, to avoid firing on benign LDAP StartTLS and similar extended operations.
- **DNS IXFR (`dns.rrtype:251`)** and **inbound DNS `RRSIG` queries**: not written. The IXFR in this capture was internal->external and ambiguous; inbound RRSIG can be legitimate DNSSEC resolver traffic.
- **Broad port-scan detection on failed flows**: not written. Most failed-flow volume to ports 6881/5190 was internal-to-internal and would have failed the widened test as known-good.
- **HTTP `%@` repeated format-string URI**: observed once but left as a blind spot; the four HTTP format-string rules cover the more common `%n`/`%s`/`%99999999`/`%%` variants.
